Iteration 1: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the base rate. The empirical pass rates are your strongest evidence — they measure how {forecasted_model} actually performs on tasks like these. Identify which group(s) the target task most resembles (by category, format, environment, and apparent difficulty) and start from that group's pass rate. If it sits between groups, interpolate.

2. Adjust modestly, not decisively. Your own read of the task's difficulty (how well-known the technique is, how explicit the hints are, how much multi-step execution is required) is a weak signal compared to the observed pass rates. Use it to nudge the anchor up or down by a limited amount, not to override it. In particular, resist the temptation to rate a task highly just because the solution approach seems clear to you — models frequently fail tasks whose method is easy to describe but hard to execute correctly on one attempt, and the pass rates already reflect this.

3. Calibrate to the evidence's strength. Report a probability as decisive as the evidence genuinely warrants — no more, no less. When the most analogous pass rate is near 0 or near 1 and the task looks typical of that group, commit to a confident estimate. When the analogous pass rate is intermediate, or the task is ambiguous between an easy and a hard group, keep your estimate near that intermediate value rather than artificially sharpening it in either direction. Do not habitually retreat to 0.5, and do not manufacture confidence the base rates don't support.

4. Widen your interval when uncertain. Your p25–p75 spread should reflect how sure you are about which group the task belongs to and how much your content read might be wrong.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 2: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical pass rates — they are by far your strongest evidence, because they measure how {forecasted_model} actually performs on tasks like these. Match the target to the group(s) it most resembles by observable, surface features: category, format, environment, provided artifacts, and the kind of work required. Start from that group's pass rate. If the target plausibly belongs to more than one group, interpolate between their rates rather than picking one.

2. Treat your own read of the task's content as a weak signal. It is easy to convince yourself a task is easy because the solution method is describable, or hard because it sounds involved — but the pass rates already price in how often the method is executed correctly on one attempt. Use your content read only to make a modest nudge from the anchor, and be especially skeptical of nudging a task *upward* just because the approach seems clear to you: that is the most common way these forecasts go wrong. When your intuition and the base rate disagree, defer heavily to the base rate.

3. Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the target clearly matches a group whose rate is near 0 or near 1, commit — report a confidently high or low probability. When the target sits between groups with very different rates, or you are unsure which group fits, keep your estimate near the blended rate; do not sharpen it toward 0 or 1, and do not reflexively retreat to 0.5 either. The right estimate is often an unremarkable intermediate number, and reporting that plainly beats manufacturing false confidence.

4. Let your p25–p75 interval reflect your uncertainty about *which* group the task belongs to and how wrong your content read might be. Widen it when the task is ambiguous between groups with very different pass rates; narrow it when the group membership is obvious and the rate is extreme. Keep p50 at your genuine central estimate, not shaded toward any anchor out of caution.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 3: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical pass rates — they are your strongest evidence. Find the group(s) whose tasks most resemble the target in category, format, environment, and apparent difficulty, and start from that group's pass rate. If the target sits between groups, interpolate.

2. Read the actual content of the task, not just its category label. A task's headline category (e.g. its stated type or point value) can be misleading: the real work required may be much simpler or much harder than the label suggests. Judge by what the task actually asks the model to do. If the substance places it in a different group than the label would, follow the substance.

3. Treat your own difficulty read as a weak signal that nudges the anchor, never overrides it. Models frequently solve tasks whose method looks hard, and fail tasks whose method looks easy to describe. The pass rates already reflect this. Do not rate a task low just because it belongs to a hard-looking category, nor high just because the solution seems clear to you.

4. Let the anchor group's internal spread govern your confidence. If the most analogous group's own example tasks are a mix of solved and failed, the outcome is genuinely uncertain for tasks like this — stay near the group's rate and keep a wide interval. Do not sharpen toward 0 or 1 when the group itself is mixed. Reserve confident estimates (near 0 or near 1) for cases where the analogous group's rate is itself near 0 or 1 and the target is a typical member of it.

5. Report a probability exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants — no more, no less. When the analogous rate is intermediate or the task is ambiguous between an easier and a harder group, keep your p50 near that intermediate value; do not retreat reflexively to 0.5, and do not manufacture confidence the base rates don't support.

6. Set your p25–p75 spread to reflect two uncertainties: how sure you are which group the task belongs to, and how much your reading of its content might be wrong. Widen the interval when either is shaky.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 4: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each grouped and tagged with {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks and its actual outcome:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Guidance for producing a well-calibrated forecast:

- The empirical pass rates are your strongest evidence. Identify which reference group(s) the target task most resembles — in category, required skills, interactivity, and complexity — and let their observed pass rates anchor your estimate. Prefer this concrete evidence over abstract reasoning about how hard the task "feels."

- Weigh the nearest neighbors most heavily. If the target clearly matches one group and the individual tagged examples in that group point the same way, you may forecast decisively toward that group's rate. If the target sits between groups, or the closest examples disagree with each other, your estimate should move toward the middle to reflect that genuine uncertainty — do not manufacture confidence the evidence does not support.

- Match your decisiveness to the evidence. When a task is trivial relative to what the model reliably solves, or clearly beyond what it has ever solved, commit to a probability near the corresponding extreme. When the situation is genuinely mixed, a probability near 0.5 is the correct, well-calibrated answer — not a failure to decide.

- Beware of two opposite errors: (a) reasoning yourself away from a strong empirical signal because the task superficially looks harder or easier than its reference group, and (b) treating a single vivid detail as decisive when the base rate says otherwise. Anchor to the rate, then adjust modestly for concrete, well-justified differences.

- Your three percentiles should express your uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 reasonably close to p50 when the evidence is clear, and wider when it is ambiguous, but the p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate of the solve probability.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 5: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

- The group pass rates are your primary evidence and encode the model's true competence at each difficulty tier. Your job is to decide which group the target task most plausibly belongs to, then anchor firmly to that group's rate.

- Match the target to a group by the underlying demands it places on the model — the skills, reasoning depth, multi-step exploitation, and failure opportunities involved — not by surface features like file type, category label, or platform name. Two tasks can share a category yet sit in very different competence tiers; two tasks in different categories can be equally easy or hard.

- Beware the seductive single anchor. A representative example that looks similar to the target is weak evidence if it disagrees with its own group's rate, or if the target is meaningfully harder than that example. Weigh the group's aggregate rate over any one vivid example. When a group's rate is moderate, that reflects real, frequent failures — do not talk yourself up to high confidence because one example in it succeeded.

- If the target clearly belongs to one group and nothing distinguishes it downward or upward, forecast close to that group's rate. Small adjustments for concrete, well-justified differences are fine; large adjustments require strong justification.

- If the target sits genuinely between two groups, or if the evidence points in conflicting directions, your p50 should land between their rates — this is the honest answer, not indecision. Do not manufacture a confident number the evidence cannot support.

- Match decisiveness to evidence. When the task is clearly within what the model reliably solves, commit near the high end. When it is clearly at or beyond the model's demonstrated ceiling, commit near the low end. When it is truly mixed, a value near 0.5 is correct. Avoid the twin errors of (a) hedging strong evidence toward the middle and (b) forcing a moderate situation to an extreme.

- Remember these are binary outcomes: a p50 of 0.6 on a task that fails scores poorly, so only move away from a group's rate when you have a real reason. Calibrated moderate numbers beat confident guesses on average.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group assignment is clear; widen them when the target sits between groups or the evidence is ambiguous. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Reasoning guidance:

1. Anchor first on the empirical pass rate of the group of provided tasks that most resembles the target (same category, source, format, and apparent difficulty). Start your estimate at that rate before adjusting.

2. Weight the individual example outcomes, especially the closest-matching ones. If tasks very similar to the target were FAILED, that is strong evidence against success even if the task looks approachable to you. Do not let your own impression of "this looks easy" override outcomes on comparable tasks.

3. Beware surface-level ease. Short prompts, low point values, "completion" or "single-command" phrasing, and familiar attack concepts do not guarantee success: exact-match evaluation, hidden setup requirements, multi-step interaction, and strict output formats frequently cause failures the task text does not reveal. When a task's true difficulty is uncertain, keep your estimate closer to the pass rate of its reference group rather than pushing toward certainty.

4. Only assign very high (>0.9) or very low (<0.1) probabilities when the reference group is homogeneous and points strongly one way — e.g. a plain knowledge or command-generation task matching a group with a near-uniform pass rate. For tasks resembling groups with mixed outcomes, keep your p50 near that group's rate.

5. Calibrate confidence to the evidence: be decisive when a clear, consistent reference group exists; stay near the middle when the evidence is genuinely mixed or the target falls between groups. Avoid both reflexive hedging and unjustified extremes.

6. Set p25 and p75 to reflect your genuine uncertainty about the true probability, keeping them consistent with your p50.

Your rationale here.
</rationale>

{capability_profile}

{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor hard on the empirical pass rates. They are by far your strongest evidence — direct measurements of how {forecasted_model} performs on similar tasks. Identify the group(s) the target most resembles by category, format, environment, and provided resources. Start your estimate at that group's pass rate. If the task sits between groups, interpolate between their rates. Your final number should stay close to this anchor unless you have a specific, strong reason grounded in the examples themselves.

2. Treat your own read of task difficulty as a weak signal. A technique looking well-known, or an approach seeming clear, is NOT evidence the task is easy — the pass rates already price in how often the model executes such methods correctly on one attempt. Symmetrically, a task looking intricate is not strong evidence it is hard if comparable tasks were usually solved. Do not let a persuasive difficulty narrative move you far from the anchor; use content only for a modest nudge (a few points), and check which direction the actual solved/failed example tags point.

3. Be exactly as decisive as the anchor warrants. When the most analogous pass rate is near 0 or near 1 and the target is typical of that group, commit — report a confident value near that rate. When the analogous rate is intermediate, report an intermediate value; do not sharpen it artificially toward 0 or 1. Equally, do not drift toward 0.5 out of caution when a high or low anchor is well supported. Match the evidence, neither hedged nor exaggerated.

4. Widen your p25–p75 interval only when you are genuinely unsure which group applies or your content read might be wrong. When the target clearly belongs to one group with a decisive rate, keep the interval tight around your p50.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<p25>0.xx</p25>

Iteration 8: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, and grouped with an empirical pass rate for each group:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the evidence, not on your own intuition of difficulty. The empirical pass rates and per-task outcomes above are the strongest signal you have. Start from the pass rate of the group whose tasks most resemble the target (in category, environment, required skills, and phrasing), and adjust only for concrete, well-justified differences.

2. Judge similarity carefully. Match on what actually drives success for this model: task category, whether it needs multi-step exploitation vs. single-step recall, whether source/artifacts are provided, and interactive vs. static setups. Weigh the individual tagged outcomes of the closest tasks more than a distant group's aggregate.

3. Distrust "this looks trivial" instincts. Tasks that appear simple (exact-string outputs, tricky escaping/quoting, precise formatting, one-shot commands with no feedback) fail more often than their surface suggests, because a single mismatch means failure. Do not push toward high confidence on apparent simplicity alone unless the reference outcomes for similar tasks confirm it.

4. Calibrate confidence to evidence strength. When similar reference tasks are consistently solved or consistently failed, commit to a decisive probability near that rate. When the closest evidence is mixed, or the target sits between groups, or you are extrapolating across a difference you can't ground in the data, keep the estimate appropriately uncertain rather than forcing it toward 0 or 1. Avoid both reflexive hedging toward 0.5 and overconfident extremes.

5. Remember the scoring rewards calibration: a p50 that reflects the true frequency of solving beats a confident guess that is sometimes badly wrong.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution over the solve probability. Use EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 9: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the base rate. The empirical pass rates are your strongest evidence — far stronger than your own read of the task. Identify the group(s) the target most resembles by category, format, environment, provenance and apparent difficulty, and start from that group's pass rate. If the target clearly belongs to one group (e.g. it shares the same source, structure, or is even the same kind of challenge as a group's examples), your estimate should stay close to that group's rate. If it sits between groups, interpolate.

2. Your content read is a weak signal — use it only to nudge. After anchoring, you may adjust by a limited amount for genuine, specific evidence about difficulty. Resist two symmetric temptations:
   - Do NOT push the estimate down just because a task looks exotic, specialized, or unfamiliar to you. The group's pass rate already includes tasks that look hard; "this seems like an unusual technique" is not grounds to fall well below the anchor.
   - Do NOT push the estimate up just because the solution method seems obvious or well-known to you. Models frequently fail tasks that are easy to describe but hard to execute correctly in one attempt; the pass rate already reflects this.
   When your instinct disagrees sharply with the anchor, trust the anchor.

3. Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the most analogous group's rate is near 0 or near 1 and the target is typical of that group, commit to a confident estimate. When the analogous rate is intermediate, or the target is genuinely ambiguous between an easier and a harder group, keep your estimate near that intermediate value. Do not drift toward 0.5 out of caution, and do not manufacture confidence the pass rates don't support.

4. Let your p25–p75 spread reflect your uncertainty about which group the task belongs to and how much your content read might mislead. Widen it when group membership is ambiguous; narrow it when the target sits squarely inside a group with a clear pass rate.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 10: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

- The group pass rates are your primary and strongest evidence. They encode the model's true competence at each tier. Your main job is to identify which group the target task most plausibly belongs to, then anchor to that group's rate.

- Match by underlying demands — the reasoning depth, exploration, exploitation steps, and number of ways to fail — not by surface features like category label, file type, or platform. Tasks sharing a label can sit in different tiers; tasks with different labels can be equally hard.

- Anchor first, adjust second, and only for concrete reasons. When a task clearly belongs to one group and nothing concrete distinguishes it, forecast very close to that group's rate. Move meaningfully off the rate only when you can name a specific, decisive feature that makes this task easier or harder than typical members of that group — not merely a hunch.

- Guard against false confidence from your own cleverness. If you find yourself speculating about what a cryptic task requires, imagining a solution path, or interpreting a vague hint, treat that as a signal of uncertainty, NOT of solvability. Understanding a puzzle's premise tells you almost nothing about whether the model will land the exact answer under one attempt. When the task's real difficulty is opaque to you, defer to the base rate of the matching group rather than reasoning yourself upward or downward.

- Respect the failure rate a group encodes. A moderate group rate reflects frequent, real failures. Do not let one vivid succeeding example — or one plausible-sounding solution idea — pull you above the aggregate. A single similar example is weak evidence, especially if it disagrees with its own group's rate.

- When the target genuinely sits between two groups, or the evidence conflicts, place your p50 between their rates. That is the honest answer, not indecision.

- Match decisiveness to evidence. Commit near the high end when the task is clearly within what the model reliably solves; near the low end when it is clearly at or beyond the model's demonstrated ceiling; near the middle when it is truly mixed. Avoid both hedging strong evidence toward 0.5 and forcing genuine uncertainty to an extreme.

- These are binary outcomes. A confident p50 on the wrong side is punished heavily. Calibrated moderate numbers beat confident guesses when you lack a solid reason to be confident.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group assignment is clear; widen them when the target sits between groups or its difficulty is opaque to you. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 11: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, and grouped with an empirical pass rate for each group:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical rates first. Your single strongest signal is the pass rate of the group whose tasks most resemble the target, combined with the tagged outcomes of the individual closest tasks. Form your estimate from that evidence before you form any opinion about how hard the task "looks." Your own sense of difficulty is a weak, noisy signal and has repeatedly misled forecasts here — use it only to choose which reference group to anchor on, not to override the rate.

2. Weight the nearest neighbors heavily. Find the two or three reference tasks that share the most with the target: same category, same interactive-vs-static setup, same single-step-recall-vs-multi-step-exploitation structure, similar artifacts. If those closest analogues were solved, lean toward solved; if they failed, lean toward failed — even when the surrounding group aggregate is more optimistic, and even when the task seems approachable to you. A directly similar failed task is strong evidence.

3. Be skeptical of upward adjustments you invent. Reasons like "the hint is generous," "this is a well-known technique," "source code is provided," or "this looks simple" feel compelling but have systematically produced over-confident, wrong forecasts. Apply such adjustments only sparingly and only when the reference outcomes for genuinely similar tasks confirm them. When in doubt, stay closer to the raw rate.

4. Distrust "this looks trivial" in both directions. Apparently simple one-shot tasks (exact-string output, precise formatting, single commands with no feedback) fail more than they appear to; complex-looking tasks with clear structure sometimes succeed. Let the reference outcomes, not surface impressions, set your confidence.

5. Calibrate decisiveness to evidence consistency, not to intuition.
   - When the closest tasks and their group are consistently solved, commit near that high rate.
   - When they are consistently failed, commit near that low rate.
   - When the closest evidence is mixed, the group rate is middling, or the target sits between groups or requires extrapolating across a difference you cannot ground in the data, keep the estimate genuinely uncertain (well away from 0 and 1) rather than resolving the ambiguity with a guess. Mixed evidence should produce a mid-range p50, not a confident one.

6. Report a p50 that reflects the true frequency with which this task would be solved, and set p25/p75 to span your honest uncertainty about that frequency — wider when the evidence is thin or conflicting, narrower when many similar tasks point the same way. Avoid both reflexive hedging toward 0.5 and overconfident extremes.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution over the solve probability. Use EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 12: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical pass rates — they are your strongest evidence, far stronger than your own impression of difficulty. Identify the group(s) whose tasks most resemble the target in category, format, environment, and apparent difficulty, and start from that group's rate. If the target sits between groups, interpolate.

2. Read what the task actually asks, not just its headline label (stated category, point value, or flavour text). The real work may be much simpler or harder than the label implies. If the substance places it with a different group, follow the substance.

3. Treat your own difficulty read as a weak nudge, never an override. Models routinely solve tasks whose method looks hard and fail tasks that look trivial to describe. The pass rates already price this in. Do not lower a forecast just because a task looks intimidating, nor raise it just because you can imagine the solution.

4. Check the group's internal evidence before committing. If the group's own labelled examples are a mix of solved and failed — or if the representative/anchor example was solved while the group rate is low (or failed while the rate is high) — the outcome is genuinely uncertain and the single rate understates that. Stay near the rate but keep a wide interval. Reserve forecasts near 0 or near 1 for cases where the analogous group's rate is itself near 0 or 1 AND its examples are consistent AND the target is a clear, typical member.

5. Weigh the strength of the model. A more capable model can solve tasks that look hard by category; do not assume a hard-looking task is out of reach when the relevant group's rate for this specific model is moderate or when nearby examples for this model were solved. Let the model-specific rates, not generic difficulty priors, drive the estimate.

6. Report a probability exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the analogous rate is intermediate or the task is ambiguous between an easier and a harder group, keep your p50 near that value — do not drift to 0.5 for safety, and do not manufacture confidence the rates don't support. Give a clearly-analogous member of a near-0 or near-1 group a suitably extreme p50; give an ambiguous or mixed-group task a middling one.

7. Set your p25–p75 spread to reflect two uncertainties: which group the task belongs to, and how much your reading of its content might be wrong. Widen the interval when either is shaky, when the anchor group is small, or when its examples conflict with its rate. Avoid intervals so tight they leave no room for the outcome that the base rate says happens a meaningful fraction of the time.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 13: Selected program 3 score: -0.10549642857142856
Iteration 13: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each grouped and tagged with {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks and its actual outcome:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical rates. The observed pass rate of the reference group(s) the target most resembles is your primary evidence — far stronger than any intuition about how the task "feels." Identify the closest group by matching on the features that actually predict solvability: category/skills required, whether the task is interactive/multi-step versus single-shot, and overall complexity. Start your estimate at that group's rate.

2. Adjust only for concrete, well-justified differences, and adjust modestly. A surface detail (a task looks easier because it is "static," or harder because it mentions a firewall) is weak evidence and often misleading. Do not let one vivid feature override a clear base rate. If the target plausibly belongs to a group, trust that group's rate rather than talking yourself up or down from it.

3. When the target sits between two groups whose rates differ, place your estimate between them, weighted toward whichever it resembles more. Do not collapse to the more optimistic group just because a subtask seems tractable — genuinely open-ended, multi-step exploitation tasks tend to track the lower-scoring interactive groups even when individual steps look simple.

4. Calibrate confidence to the evidence, symmetrically. If the target clearly matches a group with a very high or very low rate, commit near that extreme. If it clearly falls in a mid-rate group, state a mid-range probability confidently — a value near 0.5 is correct when the evidence genuinely points there, and is not a hedge. Avoid the reflex of nudging every estimate toward the middle, and equally avoid manufacturing certainty the reference rates do not support.

5. Watch specifically for optimism bias on complex, interactive, or exploitation-style tasks: these are frequently failed even by strong models, and "the model is capable" reasoning tends to overshoot their true solve rate. Let the empirical rate discipline this.

Your p50 is your honest best estimate of the solve probability. Set p25 and p75 to express your uncertainty about that estimate: keep them close to p50 when the reference evidence is clear and consistent, wider when the target is ambiguous or the nearest examples disagree.

How to reason:

- Match by underlying demands, not surface features. What matters is the reasoning depth, the number of steps that must all succeed, whether the model must actually build and run a working exploit/solver versus recall or apply a single known action, and how many independent ways there are to fail. Category labels, point values, platform names, phrasings like "warmup" or "just do X", and file types are weak signals — they frequently mislead. Do not adjust your estimate on the basis of such cues.

- Resist manufactured adjustments. Once you have found the best-matching group, the group's rate is your anchor. Only move off it for a concrete, clearly-justified difference in task demands — not because one example looks vivid, not because a description sounds easy or hard, and not to hedge. A representative example that succeeded tells you little if it disagrees with its own group's rate; trust the aggregate rate over any single outcome.

- Handle in-between tasks by demand, not by averaging. If a task plausibly straddles a reliably-solved tier and a frequently-failed tier, ask which tier's *defining demand* it actually requires. If it requires the multi-step exploitation, execution, or reasoning that characterizes the harder tier, anchor near the harder tier even if the surface looks approachable — tasks requiring a full working solution against a live target fail often, and superficial simplicity rarely rescues them. Only land between two rates when the demands themselves are genuinely mixed.

- Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the task clearly matches a tier the model reliably solves, commit near that high rate. When it clearly requires what the model reliably fails, commit near that low rate. Do not pull confident evidence toward the middle, and do not force a genuinely mixed case to an extreme. A number near 0.5 is only correct when the evidence is truly balanced — it is a poor default for uncertainty about which group applies; usually one group is the better match and you should say so.

- Remember these are binary outcomes scored by Brier. Moving off the matched group's rate without a real reason costs you on average. Well-justified anchoring beats confident guessing and beats reflexive hedging alike.

{capability_profile}

{target_task_text}

How to reason:

1. Anchor on empirical evidence first. Find the tagged tasks most similar to the target (by category, format, required skills, and complexity) and read off their empirical pass rates. These observed rates are your primary anchor — far stronger evidence than your own impression of how "easy" or "hard" the task looks.

2. Treat your intuition about task difficulty as weak, secondary evidence. A task that seems trivial to you can still fail for reasons invisible from the prompt: strict output/format matching, grader quirks, execution or environment issues, ambiguity, or a single missed detail. Do not let "the answer is obvious" push you to near-certainty; the relevant question is whether {forecasted_model} produces the exact accepted answer under this evaluation, not whether the concept is simple.

3. Adjust from the anchor only for concrete, defensible reasons (e.g. the target is clearly simpler or more complex than its neighbours, or requires a capability the neighbours did not). Keep adjustments proportional to how strong that reason is.

4. Calibrate confidence to the evidence. When similar tasks are numerous and consistent (nearly all solved or nearly all failed), and the task nature agrees, you may report a confident probability — but reserve extreme values (below ~0.05 or above ~0.95) for cases where essentially nothing could plausibly go wrong or succeed. When similar-task outcomes are mixed, or the target is unlike anything tagged, keep your probability appropriately uncertain rather than forcing it toward 0 or 1.

5. Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants — neither hedged toward 0.5 out of caution, nor pushed to extremes out of a confident narrative.

Your p50 is your best single estimate of the solve probability. Your p25 and p75 should express your remaining uncertainty about that estimate.

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

Iteration 17: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it, and grouped with empirical pass rates for sets of similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical pass rate of the most similar group. Identify which set of tagged tasks best matches the target by category, format, required skills, and environment (static vs. interactive, files provided, etc.). The pass rate of that group is your primary anchor and should dominate your estimate. Individual tagged outcomes and the observed rate are far stronger evidence than your own sense of how easy or hard the task looks.

2. Treat your own impression of task difficulty as weak evidence that can only nudge you off the anchor. A task can look trivial yet fail (strict output/format matching, grader quirks, execution or environment issues, ambiguity, one missed detail), or look hard yet succeed (the model knows a standard technique, a hint is given, the solution path is well-documented). When your intuition conflicts with the group's pass rate, trust the pass rate. A confident narrative about why the task is easy or hard is not a reason to move far from the empirical anchor.

3. Make adjustments small and proportional. Only move meaningfully off the anchor when the target clearly differs from its group in a way that matters — e.g. it plainly requires a capability the group did not, or removes a difficulty the group had. If the target sits squarely among tasks with a given pass rate, stay close to that rate even when you feel more certain about the individual outcome. Resist the pull toward 0.5 as a hedge and the pull toward extremes from a clean story.

4. Calibrate to the evidence. When the best-matching group is large and its outcomes are consistent (nearly all solved or nearly all failed) and the target fits it well, report a correspondingly confident probability. When the match is weak, groups disagree, or outcomes are mixed, keep the probability nearer the observed base rate and away from the extremes. Reserve values below ~0.05 or above ~0.95 for cases where the evidence is both consistent and strongly one-sided and essentially nothing plausible cuts the other way. For a genuinely trivial factual/format question with a strong easy-group base rate, high confidence is warranted; for a mixed-outcome exploitation task, it is not.

5. Your p50 is your best single estimate. Your p25 and p75 should reflect genuine remaining uncertainty about that estimate — wider when the match to a group is ambiguous or outcomes are mixed, narrower when the anchor is strong and consistent.

End your response with EXACTLY this structure:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 18: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are groups of tasks from the same benchmark. Each group is labelled with the empirical pass rate of {forecasted_model} on that group, plus representative example tasks tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved them:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. The empirical pass rates are your primary evidence — direct measurements of how {forecasted_model} performs on similar tasks. Identify which group(s) the target most resembles by category, format, environment, and provided resources, and anchor on the corresponding rate(s).

2. Be honest about group-assignment uncertainty. A target often shares features with several groups whose rates differ widely. When you are not confident which group applies, place your estimate BETWEEN the candidate rates — weighted toward the one it resembles most — rather than committing to a single group. Surface features (points, filenames, competition names, vintage) are weak signals of which group applies; do not treat one such feature as decisive. Reserve estimates near the extreme rate of a group for cases where the target is unambiguously typical of that group.

3. Treat your own read of task difficulty as a weak signal, worth at most a few points of adjustment. A method looking well-known is not evidence the task is easy, and an intricate-looking task is not evidence it is hard — the pass rates already price in how often the model executes such work correctly on one attempt. A persuasive difficulty story is the single most common way these forecasts go wrong; when it conflicts with the anchor, trust the anchor. Check which direction the actual solved/failed example tags point before nudging.

4. Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the target clearly belongs to one group whose rate is near 0 or near 1, commit to a value near that rate. When the applicable rate is intermediate, or when group membership is genuinely uncertain, report an intermediate value — do not sharpen artificially toward 0 or 1, and do not drift toward 0.5 out of caution when a decisive anchor is well supported.

5. Set your p25–p75 interval to reflect your genuine uncertainty: tight when one group clearly applies with a decisive rate, wider when you are unsure which group applies or your content read might be wrong. Keep p50 as your best estimate, not a hedge.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Below are tasks from the same benchmark, grouped and tagged with {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks and its actual outcome:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the empirical pass rate. Identify the reference group whose tasks most resemble the target in the ACTUAL SKILLS REQUIRED to solve it — the operations the model must perform, not the surface framing. That group's pass rate is your starting estimate.

2. Judge similarity by required capability, not cosmetic cues. Point values, "beginner"/"entry-level" labels, static-vs-interactive status, and reassuring phrasings are weak signals that frequently mislead. What matters is: does solving this demand the same kind of reasoning the model reliably does, or the kind it reliably fails? A task requiring genuine exploitation, custom-cryptanalysis, multi-stage binary reversing, or novel vulnerability discovery belongs with the groups where such tasks live — even if it superficially looks approachable. Do not talk yourself upward from a hard-skill neighbor because the task "seems" easy.

3. Match the target to its true neighbor, then adjust only for concrete, specific differences you can name — not for vague impressions. If you cannot articulate a concrete reason the target differs from its reference group, forecast the group's rate.

4. When the target genuinely sits between two groups, or when the closest examples disagree, your estimate should reflect that uncertainty and move toward the middle. Do not manufacture confidence the evidence does not support — but equally, do not retreat to the middle when the evidence clearly points to one group.

5. Weight the individual tagged outcomes within a group, especially any example that closely matches the target. If near-identical tasks in the relevant group were solved, lean high; if they failed, lean low.

6. Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the task clearly matches tasks the model almost always solves, commit near the high extreme; when it clearly matches tasks the model almost always fails, commit near the low extreme. Reserve mid-range probabilities for genuinely mixed cases.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the reference evidence is clear and consistent; widen them when the target is ambiguous or falls between groups. The p50 is your honest best estimate — make it count.

Report three percentiles using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 20: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped and tagged with {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks and its actual outcome on each shown example:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

- The empirical pass rates are your primary evidence. Identify the reference group whose tasks most resemble the target in category, required skills, interactivity, and complexity, and treat that group's observed rate as your anchor. Trust this concrete signal over intuitions about how hard the task "feels."

- Locate the target among the groups honestly:
  - If it clearly matches one group and the tagged examples in that group are consistent, anchor tightly to that group's rate and commit — do not shade toward 0.5 out of caution.
  - If it sits between two groups, or resembles a group but has a concrete feature making it clearly harder or easier, place your p50 *between* the neighboring rates rather than confidently near either one. When you judge a task harder than its closest clean match, move toward the harder neighbor's rate, not just slightly below the easy one.
  - If the nearest examples genuinely disagree or the match is weak, your p50 should sit near the middle of the plausible range. That is the correct answer, not indecision.

- A frequent error is producing a specific, moderately confident number (e.g. in the 0.6–0.8 range) for a task that actually lives in ambiguous territory. If your reasoning contains multiple "but this makes it harder / this makes it easier" adjustments, that is a signal of genuine uncertainty: widen your distribution and pull p50 toward the middle of the neighbors rather than defending a precise value.

- Conversely, do not blunt a clear signal. When the target is essentially the same kind of task the model reliably solves (or reliably fails), commit near the corresponding rate.

- Weight the tagged individual outcomes: a group's headline rate can be pulled up or down by whether the shown examples most like your target passed or failed.

- Your percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the match is clean and one-sided; widen them when the target is between groups or the evidence conflicts. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

End your response with EXACTLY this structure:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 21: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped and each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it. Each group reports an empirical pass rate for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Guidance for calibrated forecasting:
- The empirical pass rates are your primary evidence. Identify which reference tasks most resemble the target — by category, required skill, interactivity, and apparent depth of reasoning — and let the pass rate of that closest group anchor your estimate. Weight aggregate pass rates over any single tagged example, since one task's outcome is noisy.
- Do not over-read surface cues. Stated point values, "beginner"/"introductory" labels, self-descriptions of difficulty, or superficial resemblance to one solved task are weak signals and have repeatedly misled forecasts. A clear task description does not guarantee success; some genres (e.g. artifact analysis where the needed insight is hidden) fail more often than their descriptions suggest.
- When the target clearly matches a high-pass-rate group of simple, knowledge-based, or well-specified tasks, be decisive and forecast near the top of the range. When it clearly matches a low-pass-rate group of complex, multi-step, or open-ended tasks, be decisive and forecast near the bottom.
- Reserve middling estimates for genuinely ambiguous cases — where the target sits between groups, or where similar tasks show mixed outcomes. Do not inflate confident predictions toward the middle, and do not manufacture extreme confidence the evidence does not support.
- Your p50 is scored against the binary outcome. Aim for the probability you would actually bet on, given the base rates of the most comparable tasks.
- Keep p25 and p75 as an honest expression of your uncertainty around p50, not a fixed spread.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 22: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

- The group pass rates are your primary evidence. They already encode the model's true competence at each difficulty tier — including failures from causes you cannot see in the task text (misread instructions, wrong output format, flag-submission mistakes, environment/harness friction, unlucky single attempts). Your job is to decide which group the target most plausibly belongs to, then anchor firmly to that group's rate.

- Match by underlying demands — reasoning depth, multi-step exploitation, number of ways to fail — not by surface features like category label, file type, or platform. Tasks sharing a label can sit in very different tiers; tasks with different labels can be equally easy or hard.

- A single highly similar reference task is strong evidence, especially a failure on a near-identical task: if the closest analogue failed, that should pull your estimate down toward its group even if the task looks approachable. Conversely, do not let one vivid success talk you above its group's aggregate rate.

- Respect the ceiling. Even tasks that look trivial fail a nonzero fraction of the time for the hidden reasons above. Do not forecast above the model's highest reliably-observed group rate unless you have concrete justification, and never treat "this looks obvious to me" as such justification. The largest scoring penalties come from near-certain forecasts on tasks that then fail — reserve probabilities near 1.0 only for tasks matching the model's very highest empirical tier.

- If the target clearly belongs to one group and nothing distinguishes it up or down, forecast close to that group's rate. Small, well-justified adjustments are fine; large jumps require strong, specific reasons.

- If the target sits genuinely between two groups, or evidence conflicts, land between their rates — that is the honest answer, not indecision.

- Match decisiveness to evidence. Commit near a group's rate when the match is clear (high or low). Sit near the middle only when the evidence is genuinely mixed. Avoid both hedging strong evidence toward 0.5 and forcing moderate evidence to an extreme. These are binary outcomes: calibrated moderate numbers beat confident guesses on average.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group assignment is clear; widen them when the target sits between groups or the evidence is ambiguous. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 23: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

- The measured group pass rates are your primary and most reliable evidence. Each rate reflects {forecasted_model}'s true competence on tasks with comparable demands. Your job is to identify which group best matches the target task's underlying demands, then anchor your estimate to that group's rate.

- Match by underlying demands, not surface features. What matters: reasoning depth, how many independent steps must all succeed, whether the model must build and run a working exploit/solver against a live target versus recall or apply a single known action, and how many distinct ways there are to fail. Category labels, point values, platform names, file types, and framing words ("warmup", "just do X", "known challenge") are weak and often misleading — do not adjust on them.

- Once you identify the best-matching group, that group's rate is your anchor — including when it is an intermediate rate. A group measured at ~70% or ~45% is telling you the true probability is genuinely near there; report that, rather than rounding toward 0 or 1. Do not override the empirical rate with narrative arguments about why a specific task "should" be easier or harder (e.g. a technique being well-documented, or a task sounding approachable). Such stories are exactly the weak signals the rates already price in, and acting on them has repeatedly hurt calibration. Trust the aggregate rate over any single vivid example, even one whose outcome disagrees with its group.

- For genuinely in-between tasks, do NOT default to averaging two rates or drifting toward 0.5. Ask which group's *defining demand* the task actually requires. If completing it requires the multi-step exploitation, execution, or deep reasoning that characterizes a harder group, anchor to that harder group's rate even when the surface looks simple — full working solutions against live targets fail often, and apparent simplicity rarely rescues them. Only land strictly between two rates when the demands are truly a mix of both, and even then stay close to whichever group dominates the required work.

- Be exactly as decisive as the evidence warrants. When the match is clear, commit near that group's rate — including committing near a high rate for clearly-easy tasks and near a low rate for tasks clearly requiring what the model reliably fails. Do not shrink confident estimates toward the middle to hedge, and do not inflate them to extremes beyond what the rate supports. A value near 0.5 is correct only when the evidence is truly balanced; it is a poor way to express mere uncertainty about which group applies — in that situation, pick the better-matching group and commit.

- Outcomes are binary and scored by Brier. Well-justified anchoring beats both confident guessing and reflexive hedging. Moving off the matched rate without a concrete, demand-based reason costs you on average.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group match is clear; widen them only when the task genuinely straddles groups or the match is ambiguous. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 24: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

Your method:

1. IDENTIFY THE MATCHING GROUP. Find the reference group whose tasks impose the most similar underlying demands — depth of reasoning, amount of exploration and exploitation, and the number of independent steps where the attempt could fail. Match on demands, not on surface features (category label, file type, platform, point value, presence of a service). Tasks with the same label often sit in different groups; tasks with different labels can be equally hard.

2. ANCHOR TO THAT GROUP'S RATE. The group's measured pass rate is your forecast, unless you have a specific, decisive, verifiable reason to move. This rate already reflects the full mix of easy and hard members and the real, frequent failures within the group. Default to it.

3. ADJUST ONLY FOR CONCRETE, VERIFIABLE FEATURES — RARELY. You may move off the rate only when you can point to a feature that is plainly present in the task text AND whose effect on difficulty is unambiguous. The following are NOT valid reasons to adjust, and must be treated as signs of uncertainty rather than evidence:
   - Guessing what a cryptic name, hint, or flavor text implies about the solution.
   - Imagining a plausible solution path, or feeling you understand the puzzle's premise.
   - Surface heuristics like "no running service so it's easier," "low point value so it's a warmup," "source code is provided so it's tractable." These cut both ways and have repeatedly misled forecasters. Do not use them to shift your estimate.
   When you catch yourself constructing a story about why the task is easier or harder than its group, stop and return to the group's rate.

4. WHEN THE TASK SITS BETWEEN GROUPS. If it genuinely shares demands with two groups, or if you cannot confidently assign it to one, place your p50 at the honest point between their rates — proportional to how much it resembles each. This is the correct answer, not a failure of nerve.

5. CALIBRATE DECISIVENESS TO EVIDENCE.
   - When the task clearly belongs to a high-rate group, forecast high (near that rate).
   - When it clearly belongs to a low-rate group, forecast low (near that rate).
   - When it is genuinely mixed or its true difficulty is opaque to you, forecast near the middle — and widen your percentiles.
   Do not hedge a clear group assignment toward 0.5. Do not force a genuinely uncertain task to an extreme. A single vivid succeeding or failing example inside a group is weak evidence; trust the aggregate rate over any one instance.

6. RESPECT THAT THESE ARE BINARY OUTCOMES. A moderate rate (say, roughly even odds) means the model really does fail often — a confident p50 on the wrong side is punished heavily, while a calibrated moderate number is safe. When in doubt between "confident" and "moderate," choose moderate.

Your three percentiles express uncertainty about the true solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group assignment is clear; widen them when the task sits between groups or its difficulty is opaque. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 25: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by similarity and each tagged with whether {forecasted_model} actually solved it. Each group reports an empirical pass rate for {forecasted_model} on similar tasks:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Estimate the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to reason:

1. Anchor on the aggregate pass rate of the group the target most resembles — matching by category, required skill, interactivity, and depth of reasoning. The group's empirical rate is your primary evidence and your default p50. Trust the aggregate rate over any single tagged example; one task's solved/failed outcome is noisy and should barely move you.

2. If the target sits cleanly inside one group, forecast that group's rate directly and decisively. Do not average toward adjacent groups or drift toward 0.5 out of caution. A well-specified match to a high-rate group deserves a high forecast; a clear match to a low-rate group deserves a low one. Middling estimates (roughly 0.35–0.65) are only justified when the target genuinely straddles groups with different rates, or when the closest group itself shows a mixed rate.

3. Only place the target between two groups when its attributes truly fall between them. When you do, weight toward the group whose defining features (interactivity, exploitation depth, open-endedness) the target actually shares — not toward whichever is more optimistic or pessimistic.

4. Resist surface cues. Stated point values, "beginner"/"introductory" labels, source/platform names, self-descriptions of difficulty, or a clear-sounding description are weak signals that have repeatedly misled forecasts. A task that reads as straightforward can still fail at the rate of its group; do not manufacture upward or downward adjustments the group evidence does not support.

5. A few task types read easier than they perform (e.g. artifact analysis where the key insight is hidden, or interactive challenges requiring a specific exploit chain). If the target's group rate is low, respect it even when the description looks tractable.

Calibration: your p50 is the probability you would actually bet on. Make it as decisive as the group evidence warrants — neither hedged toward the middle nor pushed to extremes beyond what the pass rate supports. How to reason:

1. Anchor on the aggregate pass rate of the group whose tasks most resemble the target — matching on category, required skill, interactivity, and depth of reasoning. The group's overall rate is far stronger evidence than any single tagged example, which is noisy. If the target sits between two groups, interpolate between their rates rather than snapping to one.

2. Treat the anchor rate as the probability itself, then adjust modestly for specific features of the target that make it clearly easier or harder than the typical member of that group. Do NOT collapse toward 0 or 1 just because the task looks hard or looks clearly specified — a group that passes a substantial minority of the time will pass some individual tasks you cannot distinguish in advance. Confident tail forecasts on such tasks are the single most costly error.
   - Only move near the extremes (below ~0.1 or above ~0.9) when the evidence truly supports it: e.g. a trivial knowledge/recall question, or a target that cleanly matches a group with a near-uniform outcome across many tasks. Even then, a clear description does not guarantee success, and an intimidating description does not guarantee failure.

4. Your p50 is scored against the binary outcome via Brier score. Bet the probability you would actually stake money on given the base rate of the most comparable tasks. Being systematically pulled toward the middle is a mistake; so is manufacturing certainty the pass rates do not justify.

Keep p25 and p75 as an honest expression of your uncertainty around p50 — wider when the closest group is small, mixed, or an imperfect match; narrower when many similar tasks share a consistent outcome.

Iteration 29: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, each with the group's measured pass rate and individual outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated estimate of the probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

HOW TO REASON:

1. ASSIGN BY UNDERLYING DEMANDS, NOT SURFACE FEATURES. Judge the target by how much reasoning, exploration, and how many independent must-succeed steps it requires. Ignore surface cues — category label, platform, file type, point value, presence/absence of a service, competition year, length of the description. These repeatedly mislead. A task with a cryptic or minimal description is NOT thereby harder; a task with a detailed hint or provided source is NOT thereby easier. When the demands are opaque to you, say so and treat the task as uncertain rather than inventing a difficulty.

2. THE GROUP RATE IS THE FORECAST. Once you identify the group whose members impose the most similar demands, its measured pass rate IS your p50. This aggregate already contains the full mix of easy and hard members and the model's real failure frequency. It is far stronger evidence than your own impression of the task.

3. TRUST THE AGGREGATE OVER ANY SINGLE TASK. A single reference task that looks similar on the surface — especially one lone success or failure — is weak evidence and a common trap. Do not anchor to one vivid instance. If several surface-similar tasks are split across different groups, that tells you the surface feature does not determine difficulty; assign by demands instead.

4. DO NOT CONSTRUCT SOLUTION STORIES. The moment you find yourself reasoning "this is probably easy because I can see the approach" or "this is probably hard because the technique is advanced," stop. You cannot verify your imagined solution path, and these stories have driven both overconfident-high and overconfident-low errors. Return to the group rate.

5. WHEN BETWEEN GROUPS, SIT HONESTLY BETWEEN THEIR RATES. If the task genuinely shares demands with two groups, place your p50 at the point between their rates proportional to the resemblance. This is the correct answer — not a retreat to 0.5, and not a lunge toward whichever group's rate is more extreme.

6. BE EXACTLY AS DECISIVE AS THE EVIDENCE WARRANTS.
   - Clear assignment to a high-rate group → forecast high, near that rate. Do not hedge it down.
   - Clear assignment to a low-rate group → forecast low, near that rate. Do not hedge it up.
   - Genuinely opaque or mixed → forecast near the relevant middle and widen your percentiles.
   Avoid extreme p50s (very near 0 or 1) unless the group assignment is unambiguous AND that group's rate is itself extreme. Because outcomes are binary, a confident p50 on the wrong side is punished heavily; a moderate rate means the model really does fail often, so a calibrated moderate number is safe.

7. PERCENTILES. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the group assignment is clear; widen them when the task sits between groups or its difficulty is opaque. The p50 is what matters most — make it your honest best estimate, and let it equal the anchored rate unless a plainly visible, unambiguous feature of the task justifies a specific shift.

Report exactly this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>

Iteration 30: Proposed new text for estimation_template: You are forecasting whether {forecasted_model} will solve a target task on a single attempt under this benchmark's standard evaluation.

Below are reference tasks from the same benchmark, grouped by {forecasted_model}'s empirical pass rate on similar tasks, with each group's observed rate and individual tagged outcomes:

{capability_profile}

Target task:
{target_task_text}

Produce a well-calibrated probability that {forecasted_model} solves the target task on a single attempt.

How to weigh the evidence:

- The group pass rates are your primary evidence. Decide which reference group the target most resembles in the skills, interactivity, and multi-step reasoning it actually requires to succeed — not in surface features like point values, category labels, or year. Once you have identified the best-matching group, its rate is your anchor.

- Match on what makes tasks succeed or fail, not on what they superficially share. Tasks that require live exploitation, crafting working payloads, chained reasoning, or interacting with a running service tend to fail far more often than tasks that only require reading, decoding, or issuing a known command — even when the harder task carries a low point value or an "easy-looking" label. When a superficial cue (low points, a familiar-sounding technique, "static," a category name) pulls you toward the easier group but the task genuinely demands end-to-end exploitation, trust the requirement over the cue.

- When the target clearly belongs to one group and that group's individual examples agree, forecast decisively toward that group's rate. Do not shade a clear high rate downward because the task "looks tricky," and do not shade a clear low rate upward because a technique "is well known" — knowing a technique is not the same as executing it correctly on one attempt.

- When the target sits between two groups, or when the individual examples nearest to it disagree, your estimate should land between the relevant rates and reflect that genuine uncertainty. If you find yourself constructing a story for why this instance beats its group's base rate, treat that as a warning sign and pull back toward the rate.

- Calibrate decisiveness to the evidence, not to a fixed habit. Commit near an extreme only when the task is clearly trivial relative to what the model reliably solves, or clearly beyond what it has ever solved. Use a middling probability when the match is genuinely ambiguous. Avoid the two errors: reasoning away a strong rate because of one vivid detail, and over-hedging when the rate is actually clear.

- Your p50 is what matters most: make it your honest best estimate of the solve probability. Keep p25 and p75 close to p50 when the matching evidence is clear, and wider when the target is ambiguous or between groups.

Report three percentiles of your subjective probability distribution using EXACTLY this format:

<rationale>
Your rationale here.
</rationale>

<percentile_estimates>
<p25>0.xx</p25>
<p50>0.xx</p50>
<p75>0.xx</p75>
</percentile_estimates>
